ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e193	I have the feeling that there is going to be a historic day, no matter what decision the Stortinget has to make today, linking both to the municipal reform, for which I am the case spokesman, and to the regional reform, which we are dealing with in parallel. It will be incorporated into a series of historical decisions starting back in 1837, when we got the presidential laws in Norway and got local self-government in the whole country. We know the history: we start the journey for local democracy with a little over 400 municipalities. Then it added up in the 1930s, when we had a maximum of 747, before one had the big last municipal reform based on the Schei Committee's treatment and position, which led to a walk from 744 to 454 municipalities when one was finished in 1967. It is the last major municipal reform carried out in Norway before this enlargement took place, and the local politicians in Norway got their wish fulfilled by a municipal reform. It is important for me to say that the initiative for a local reform has not come from the accumulation and government, but it has come from local politicians in Norway, who at the national meeting of the Labour Party, the Right, the Progress Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left have agreed to program formulations that recognise the need for a local reform. It forms the basis for this great majority to invite us to a municipal reform, which the local politicians in Norway have taken up and made a solid effort to discuss how we should equip ourselves to create strong municipalities for the future. So first of all, I want to thank local politicians in Norway for taking this opportunity and making sure to look forward and discuss how to equip yourself to provide good business and community services. We have been working with the municipal reform throughout this period, and we have had a consistent line, namely that Stortinget has a superior responsibility to ensure that Norway is properly divided. Therefore, there was a broad plurality at Stortinget that was behind the need for municipal reform and neighboring talk. The principal lines were outlined in the 2015 local authority proposal setting, where one said: ‘Derogations from this principle of volunteering may, however, be applicable in very special situations in which individual municipalities are unable to stop changes that are appropriate for regional reasons.’;» It has been our premise since one day, and I am very pleased that today we are presenting a position which takes care of what we have always said, that the general rule has our voluntary character, but that, in exceptional cases, the Storting can make decisions that go beyond individual municipalities, but which are well founded on regional considerations. Now, when we sum up the reform, it means that 121 municipalities are merged into 47 new municipalities, which means that we go from 428 municipalities at the beginning of this period to 354. In other words, more is happening in this ageing period – in these four years – than it has happened in 50 years before. In general, it is based on local leadership and local decisions. The only thing that has happened in the history of the last 50 years more than will happen here today is decisions across local decisions, for there was a chance to set a stronger record in the 1990s. Gunnar Berge was then the municipal minister, and Stortinget made 14 decisions across local decisions. It was probably just the decisions one made in the municipal reform. So today we're making fewer, because this reform is based on volunteering. We want to make sure that 32 pst. by the inhabitants of Norway will have new municipalities to live in from 1 January 2020, 1,68 million Nordics will then live in a new municipality. In addition to those who have now found each other and were some about a new municipality, there are many municipalities that have been positive. A total of 156 municipalities have said that they want to become a new municipality. Nearly two thirds of the people of Norway live in a municipality that has been positive. When we look at the party colors locally, we see that there are not the same dividing lines there as there is in this room in the municipal reform. We see that Center Party rapporteurs – in fact, a quarter of them – live in a municipality that will be new from 1 January 2020. We see that 68 Labour Party spokesmen have voted for their municipality to be part of a new municipality, and we see that spokesmen in all parties have taken leadership. So locally, we see that all party colors have contributed to the creation of a stronger municipal structure. It's interesting to register. We also have a proposition today that goes on tasks for the municipalities. For the Progress Party, it is important to move tasks down to where people live in the district. The services are supposed to be close to people. We are dedicated to giving new tasks and more trust in municipalities. Maybe trust is the most important thing. I myself have been our spokesperson for ten years and experienced then – I should not say lack of trust, but that the state had a great need to review local decisions by the statesmen. It was still contradictions, etc. From County. There's a dramatic change with this government, which gives confidence. We look at the statistics, mainly the county officials won things in the red-greens' time, but now under this government local democracy wins most of the complaints of the department. So we've given municipalities more than one task and more trust. Regional reform will also be addressed today. There I must be clear that it is primarily the Christian People's Party and the Left who are keen for a regional reform and who have brought us with us subsidized to a regional reform. It's no surprise to anyone that the Progress Party ideally thinks that a country with a little over five million people could cope with strong municipalities and the state. We can do without the rule, but it wasn't a plurality for that line, and then we have chosen to contribute rather to having about 10 regions or districts instead of today's 19, with the reason that we'll get less money for administration and for politicians' wages, which we'll follow up through this regional reform. A little bit of clarification for us to put in a loose proposal on behalf of the four parties so that the district division in North Norway will also be drawn today. It was originally in the setting of the municipality proposal, but the cleanest thing to do is to take the whole regional reform today. That's why there's a loose proposal on the table today. Finally, I would like to say that it is an important day for Kommune-Noreg. It shows interest in the community reform. The reason why we're doing that, we have to remind ourselves a little bit. It's not because it's important in itself to reach a certain number, but it's important to make sure that we have the right division of Norway to deliver good services, especially for those who need it the most. These are those who need specialized services from their local authorities, we think the most about when we discuss community reform. They're not the kind of people who want to write reading articles and stand high on the barricades, but they're the ones who need special services every day. We've had the most focus on. They depend on a professional environment that can solve their problems. It'll be too vulnerable if you only have one subject. We know that for the past 50 years, Kommune-Noreg has had a lot of new, demanding tasks. In the future, they will have even more important tasks to solve for the inhabitants. Then we are forced to ensure that we can have a large, strong professional environment that takes care of the inhabitants and those who need the most. Nor is it a secret that there has been a lot of traffic in recent years. A new national transport plan will take place even more so that the distances between municipalities have been smaller. There's a lot of money happening on the investment side, which means that the municipal boundaries that you had in the 1960s are not the right ones for the next 50 years. People who live in our country will be sure that we have a responsibility to make sure they get good services. We are still strengthening the local economy, but it is also a matter of ensuring that we have divided Norway as the local politicians have asked us to do, and as the local politicians have gone ahead to recommend the municipal structure on which the Stortinget is invited to decide. So we're going from 428 to 354 municipalities. I'm very happy with the work done in this period. Thanks to local leadership, we get a local reform and a municipal structure that provides the basis for delivering good services to our citizens. It's a good interaction between big business, government and local democracy that has led us here by putting good carrots at our disposal, and then has a local-based leadership. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e313	When I was elected to the Stortinge for the second time almost four years ago, I had a clearly defined goal. That goal was to ensure the penetration of a regional reform that would bring more power out of this hall and more power out to the states of the country. I wanted to see a revitalization of the intermediate level, to see it flourish, as living meeting places where policies are designed and decisions made. The county councils should no longer be empty shells without the right and power to take their own decisions, nor should they be merely an instance of consultation documents. The county councils must become an important political arena where decisions are taken – and there are decisions that count. It's not without reason I've shown such commitment to the regional reform. I have seen for myself how little county self-government exists, how little freedom the county municipalities have to do tasks in the way they think is useful to their own inhabitants. When we send large-scale representatives who limit the role of county councils, they always do with the best intentions: this is too important to leave to county councils, this must be coordinated for the whole country. This is how we have demolished the county councils for an extension of state power. It can't be like that anymore, and I'm glad we're saying today that enough is enough. Centralization stops here. Several governments have tried a regional reform in the past, but among the cooperation parties only the Left and Christian People's Party wanted a further third. The two government parties, the Right and the Advance Party, would primarily abolish the county municipalities as a whole. This doesn't make it any less incredible that we have achieved the masterpiece that today we are carrying out the greatest post-war municipal reform, on the layers of two parties that would really want to abolish the county councils in their entirety. Fortunately, we have something more fundamental in common. It is the belief that society is growing from below and up, not from above and down – the belief that county differences require municipal responses, the consensus that power is going down and out to the people. This brought together the bourgeois parties around the regional reform with which we have today won. In order to defend the relocation of heavy tasks to county municipalities, it was important that they become larger and more robust. The professional environment had to expand, the economy improved and the population base strengthened. That's why we agreed to redraw the County Map. As a result, we now go from 19 to 11 counties. The criticism of the map has focused on individual regions, that not all new county municipalities are optimally fused, as one wishes. These critics don't see the forest for just trees. It's the whole thing that matters, it's the whole thing that makes it possible for us to move problems down and out to the county councils. This reform has laid a foundation, and if future governments are as courageous as we have been, then there are good opportunities for us to see further perfections of the County Map in the years to come. I don't dare say anything about what changes should come in the next round – otherwise it's quick to get quoted in DN on something you haven't meant to. This has become a democracy reform, and it has also become a red tape reform. We have changed the balance of power in Norway. We've given new impetus to the regional centres. At the same time, we move tasks from the IMFI, we move tasks from Innovation Norway, and we move tasks to the regional research funds – tasks that were previously solved nationally. In the fight against paragraph guessing, detailing and one-size-fits-all solutions, we are taking a big step in the right direction today. Having adopted a reform despite the fact that my party and the Christian People's Party constitute 19 mandates in this Chamber, is in many ways a trick. That we've reduced the number of county municipalities to 11 is large. But that we're transferring such a large task portfolio to the regions as what we're doing today gives cause for pride. We could not have achieved this without the will of the government parties to seek compromise, but neither without the support of task transfer to the regions that I experience we have received from both the Workers' Party and the Centre Party. In major issues such as regional reform, such multi-party unity is even more important than usual, and I am sure that despite the rhetoric about wanting to reverse regional reform, there is hardly one party represented in the committee that wants to doubt state tasks. Based on the thin list of tasks for which we were given a majority in 2016, the list of tasks for which we have now received a majority of committees is a major step in the right direction to fulfil the intention to move power down and out to the people. It is clear that as one of the initiators of the reform, I would have liked to have transferred even more tasks to the county councils, among others. health establishments. It wasn't the majority for that. But we can't make the mistake of saying that's how it happened, and that's how it will stay. Now we have put in place the most comprehensive county reform in recent times, and then we also have to bear in mind that this is a foundation on which we can build. It's not a completed house. Regional reform, municipal reform and the relocation of state jobs have shown that it is the bourgeois parties that deliver future district policies. Had we been hiding behind old boundaries and division of tasks, the way out would have been too easy. It would be said that this is too important to leave to the county municipalities in the country. Local and county self-government, as I said, demands courage. It requires trust in our communities, in those who are chosen to represent their own communities. We know that the current structures only give good excuses for further centralization. We've seen that all too often. Today we are looking forward to a future in which the county councils flourish, as arenas for political decisions and debates. They will no longer be empty shells without the right and power to make their own decisions. Enough is enough. We're transferring far more tasks than we've ever done in county municipalities. This is district policy a simple verra-building can be proud of.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e441	Let me thank the Committee for the good cooperation on Prop. 91 L, which deals with several tasks for municipalities. The municipality is the bearer of the indigenous welfare offer. The municipality is a community developer, authority practitioner and local democracy. The municipality is the arena of the inhabitants to solve important tasks jointly, not under-use by the country. Today, we take – and we have taken – new steps throughout this ageing period that allow municipalities to provide good services and to take more responsibility for their own development. The need for a municipal reform was excessive when the Right-Fremskritsparte government joined. There was no need to assign new tasks to municipalities in order to justify the municipal reform; it could justify itself. But the Right and Government are still proposing more tasks because we think it is right that we decentralize more, that we give Norwegian municipalities, Norwegian people elected, local people elected more influence over what is happening locally. The strategy to strengthen municipalities and local self-government can be summarised in three points: better municipal economy, freer municipalities and more important municipalities. We strengthen the local economy year by year, making it easier to create better well-being locally. Freer municipalities are about giving municipalities greater freedom from state override. The municipal decisions weigh more heavily, the planning and building law simplifies the proceedings, and the local authorities' position vis-à-vis the state is strengthened. More important municipalities are given when we give the municipalities more tasks, so that more decisions can be made locally and offers that are suitable locally. That's how we fill local democracy. There is decentralisation in practice. Others talked about it for years. With the Right in Government, we implement. In addition, of course, the municipal reform, which in itself is an important contribution to creating municipalities that can be more mastery in their own house. That's what we want, isn't it? We want fewer and stronger municipalities. This will make it easier to decentralise more tasks in the future, and it will counteract the zeal to override municipalities through minimum standards from the state, legal requirements and earmarked resources. Community reform is therefore about better services for citizens, but it is also about how we can create more local democracy. But it's not really local democracy if the state overrides the municipalities most of the time they do. Then you're not the master of your own house. Lord of his own house or wife in his own house – you can say that – you will not become if the municipalities ‘outsourcer’ more and more tasks to inter-municipal and over-municipal bodies, so that the local government no longer determines the use of resources locally. It seems that there are these individual parties in this room that seem to have been raised to an ideal, namely that it is better that the municipalities and local authorities allow others to do the job separately, than that we actually build municipalities that can do the job ourselves. It is possible that this type of outsourcing from the local government is ok for the Workers' Party, the Centre Party and SV, but in the Right we want municipalities that can solve their own problems. Today, too many municipalities have to stretch their hands in the weather and say, "No, we can't do our statutory tasks, we have to ask someone else to do it." Can't it be that that's what's ideal for Kommune-Norway? Many municipalities see that their inhabitants will be best served by building a new municipality with their neighbors. Some do so because they say that they need to prepare themselves to build stronger professional environments to provide important welfare services, whether in the area of child protection or social services. Some municipalities get together because they need to improve the business environment. Some go together because out-of-date municipal boundaries create obstacles in everyday life for the most people as well as in the planning of residential and commercial space. Norway is different, and the reasons for municipal reform and aggregation vary from place to place. I am the case rapporteur for a case that has both proposals and a number of reviews showing how the Right Party Government systematically promotes proposals that will make municipalities more important to people most and to local business and society. The government parties, together with the Christian People's Party and the Left, have agreed on a number of tasks that can be transferred to the municipalities, as well as that we now decide the division and new tasks of the county municipalities. Today we give the rapporteurs and the municipalities the right of marriage. Some people flirt with it and say that this is not an important task, it does not create stronger municipalities. No, that's because you're just busy with structure. We're actually multiplying the possibility for those who want to get married, to actually be able to do it in their own local environment. It's a decentralization of the wedding offer, nothing less, for all those who are married. Those of the largest municipalities that they believe can make a better provision for public transport to the population, and who want to see it offer along with other urban developments, may have the opportunity to do so, provided, of course, that there is cooperation with the surrounding county. Does anyone really think that a municipality would want to take responsibility for public transport if it's not because they think they can create a good deal? Of course not. But the minority in this room is obviously also afraid that larger municipalities will at all show that they can. Today we decide to give the municipalities greater responsibility in the housing social area. The grant for the establishment and adaptation of housing is transferred from the state to the municipalities, while at the same time we regulate the obligation for financial assistance as a task for the municipality. So this is an example of how we both simplify the systems and at the same time strengthen the possibilities of municipalities to see the offer to their inhabitants in context and build good local offers adapted to the inhabitants. It has also been mentioned that the government has promoted – and proposes in a number of other issues and processes – further strengthening the municipalities. Let me just mention: In one matter later today, the government has proposed that dental services should become part of the health service in municipalities for young people and parents to whom the municipalities otherwise have direct responsibility for the provision of health care. Changes to the Child Protection Act give municipalities increased responsibilities. We raise the threshold of state objections in local decisions. We give the municipalities the right to apply to the state. The Planning and Building Act simplifies and lessens the procedure. Volunteering centres have become a municipal responsibility. We are strengthening efforts to digitise municipal services, which also benefits citizens. So the sum of these and many other things, which I haven't mentioned, means a greater decentralization and spread of power than I think someone in this room has been on in modern times. We often owe the red-greens, but in this context it would be totally wrong to say that the red-greens didn't get to this in their eight years, because there's little evidence that they even tried, or had ambitions in the direction that we have. It's a historic day today. In the previous major municipal reform, almost 20 years were used from the start to the decision in Stortinget. In just under three to four years, Norwegian municipalities have now ensured that almost 100 municipalities have taken voluntary merger decisions. After today's decision, it is possible that what was 428 municipalities at the beginning of the period becomes 354 municipalities when we write in 2020. The right is impressed by Norwegian municipalities, rapporteurs and local politicians – across the party borders – who have demonstrated local leadership. The red-green opposition might say that they support most of the contractions, and that's fine. But still, there's a little bit of a hollow statement. Without a reform of instruments and progress, the most reason to believe that very little would happen, and that neither the Stortinget, the municipalities nor the inhabitants had any reason to rejoice over good results today. 1.6 million people live in what is now becoming new municipalities, but there are still challenges with the municipal structure. Some will remain small and vulnerable; others will be congregated and have out-of-date borders in press areas. Therefore, there is still a need for a municipal reform in Norway. The question to be asked in the autumn elections is whether further municipal reform means that more people should be able to join together and build new municipalities, as we have now done, or whether the Workers' Party and the Centre should be given a course in which the number of municipalities and the number of counties should go up again, whether we should go from municipal merger to municipal dissolution. One thing I am sure of: the answer to the challenges of Norway and its inhabitants in the years to come is no more municipalities or municipalities. Let me finally say that in case no. 1 is not proposed. 15, from the Right, to the Wooting.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e1111	Today, the Stortinget makes a historic decision. In many parts of the country, provision is now made for better services to be provided to citizens as a result of an increase in the size and strength of municipalities. Thank you to the Committee for its thorough and thorough consideration. Since the beginning of the 1990s, the need for municipal reform has been on the political agenda. Several attempts at reform have been made, but it has stopped. After the 2013 elections, the Christian People's Party, the Left, the Advance Party and the Right agreed that a municipal reform would now take place – and we do. In the summer of 2014, I invited all the municipalities to start the chat. There has been an impressive job in the municipalities. Local politicians from all parties have worked well, and municipalities led by all the political colors have agreed to merge. It shows that the community reform is not a party political project, but a necessary reform work to ensure good services for the inhabitants where they live, now and in the future. Both elected and employees in municipalities have shown good leadership, seen 10–20 years ahead and concluded that they will be stronger with others. They have made the demanding choice. The choice is based on knowledge of what we know comes: more older, fewer workers, more advanced services and, at the same time, greater expectations from the population. This must be handled under tighter economic conditions. Someone's talking about the municipal reform being centralisation. Then they should look back. In the last 35 years, the large municipalities have had more inhabitants and the small municipalities fewer inhabitants, without a local reform. The alternative to municipal reform is not that everything is going to be what it was. No, the option is enhanced centralisation, more detailed state management and increasing differences in quality of service between municipalities. Right can't live with that. We want equivalent services where people live. We have to assume that since the last major reform in the 1960s, there have been major changes in society – the way we live, travel, work and communicate – and the municipalities have been given far more and more demanding tasks than they had before. The government will take care of the Norwegian tradition of strong welfare municipalities that have a large, local autonomy. Local reform is therefore not about changing society; local reform is about changing society. Municipal reform has also been a demonstration of local involvement and democracy, not an overrun of local democracy, as some claim. Wide-ranging involvement across the country has been invited, and it has been well organised for citizens to be heard, both through referendums and through community surveys. However, it is important to stress that in Norway we have a representative democracy, that it is the elected representatives of the local authorities who finally take the decision, and that the Stortinget has a overall responsibility for how to organise our country and our welfare. It is also the responsibility of the Grand Duchy to ensure good and equivalent services for its citizens. It is the responsibility of the population that children in need of child protection are given a good school and that a person in need of mental health care is given this, regardless of where you live. There are worrying differences between small and large municipalities. Of course, there are differences between the small and the large, but a comprehensive report from among others. Economic research centres show that the smallest municipalities are getting worse in terms of performance or content of important municipal services. They score lower on results, e.g. at school. Minor municipalities also have major recruitment problems both in primary and care schools. In a separate survey of consultants, it is clear that they assess the competence and capacity as lower than in larger municipalities, especially in health and care. In both drug and mental health, municipalities with over 15 000 inhabitants report significantly higher personnel capacity and competence than other municipalities. Without a municipal reform, these differences will continue to increase in the coming years. The same applies to important tasks related to social development. Results show that capacity in social and spatial planning and industrial development is very low in municipalities with less than 3000 inhabitants. This is important findings, because the societal development role is essential for creating growth and development in a municipality, and without this capacity it will be difficult to prevent relocation, move and work for economic development. The parties who say no to community reform have no response to how to meet these challenges. Today's proposals for mergers are largely based on voluntary and mutual decisions. This has always been the starting point of the reform. There are a few exceptions, where the decision of the Storting will go across decisions of the local government. We go from 428 to 354 municipalities in 2020 with these decisions. Nearly two out of three residents live in a municipality where the local government has decided that they want or are positive for a merger, and about one out of three inhabitants get a new municipality through the municipal reform. The government will build society from below. We want strong local communities and living local democracies where power and responsibility are as close to the decisions as possible. This is why we put greater emphasis on local self-government, for example. in the case of matters relating to spatial planning. This is why we move more tasks to municipalities – not for local government representatives to do more, but for citizens to get better services. More tasks and more powers will also make it more interesting to be a local politician. We have confidence that elected representatives of all parties throughout the country will do well for their citizens. We want societies that are created and developed from below. Several tasks for municipalities will contribute to this. The majority of the majority of the population have moved on to a popular level between the state and the municipality. The size has also decided that a regional reform will be carried out, resulting in about ten elected regions. The county structure has been solid since the 1800s. Society has been radically changed at this time. There have been several attempts at reform in the past. I hear now that someone is talking about the reversal of today's decisions. They take on a great responsibility. Now we have the opportunity to implement what has been going on for decades. To reverse the regional or local reform, there will be more unrest, more discussions and new reform measures. I believe that the proposal for a new division provides a good basis for further developing the county councils as community developers. The division provides a good balance between the different counties, more functional housing and work regions and also a basis for stronger professional environments. The government is proposing to transfer more new tasks, and we will also set up an expert committee to look at new tasks to county municipalities. I record that some people say that, yes, they are in favour of regional reform, but there should have been more tasks. Then the question is, What kind of tasks? We hear very little about that. And when the government proposes to move problems, you go against it because there's some group that protests. Now we're laying the foundation for a new municipal structure that can provide citizens with better services. We provide the basis for a new regional structure that enables regions to contribute to the development of their role as a social developer. This is a good basis for better services for citizens.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e1594	It has been said several times in this debate that this is a brand day for the national governance system, and that is. The local boats and the local boat routes with which the Schei Committee travelled as they traveled through Norway and were to draw the new Norway map are far removed. The pattern of housing has changed, new areas of employment and housing are interwoven in a completely different way, but most of all the municipal responsibility for services is far, far larger and more complex than it was in the 1960s. Let me give you an example: the community health service. It was governed by health law, and the municipality was responsible for old people's homes, preferably in cooperation with non-profit institutions serving households. Looking at the legislation today, you can see the extensive and demanding tasks the municipalities are responsible for. But that's precisely the Big One's task, based on the knowledge and information that the Big One has. The alternative to change is standstill and a form of degeneration. Let me give you an example: if we don't do anything about today's municipal structure and base the population projection of the Central Bureau of Statistics, in 2040 we will have 110 municipalities there 30 pst. the population is over 67 years of age. There will be quite strange municipalities, where very many will need care from the municipality, and very many have to work in the older sector, and these municipalities will have very poor tax bases. That's not how a big one can sit and watch and let go. The size has decided that the same welfare services are required of the same quality, no matter where you live in the Kingdom. There are in particular two reasons for changing the municipal structure. One is to enable municipalities to provide the services they are to provide, and the other is the role of municipalities as a community developer. In my county, from which I am elected, Akershus, there are 22 municipalities that operate land planning as if the neighbouring municipality did not exist. There is enormous pressure on the cultivated land, and we have analyses that show that if the municipalities pursue their own municipal plans, it will be built down three times as much cultivated land as if you were pursuing a county plan. Then you might ask, ‘ Why don’t you pursue the county plan? ’ No, because the planning and regulatory authority is located in the municipalities and is absolutely fundamental in the local self-government. There are two strategies: a red-green one, which is to preserve today's municipal structure, which over time will give a weathering and will not enable the municipalities to deliver their tasks, or the strategy that the government is adopting today, to change to preserve.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e1607	The goal of the regional reform government has been a stronger regionally elected level that takes a clearer responsibility for social development, that the tasks that will be transferred will reinforce the need for larger and more robust professional environments. The strange thing is that this obviously does not apply to dental health. Today’s Norwegian dental model has produced good results in terms of better dental health in the population, increased focus on prevention, a well-decentralised supply and strengthening of research and competence. The dental service is one of the services the population is most satisfied with. Success is based on competent dental management, large enough professional units, a high level of competence and extensive experience, providing equivalent dental services in cities and countries. Today's organisation also provides good interaction with the municipal health service. Of the 130 hearings – the exposure has been strong in this area – only 20 municipalities were in favour of taking over the responsibility for dental health. All disciplines, such as the Dental Association, the Dental Association, the Medical Association, the Health Directorate, the Odontological Faculty in Oslo and heavy organisations such as the KS, the Trade Union, the Academics and the Parats, do not recommend a transfer of dental health to the municipalities. The Labour Party considers that it is alarming that the government is still choosing to propose such a transfer, which can neither rely on heavy professional or organisational arguments nor offer a better offer to those who need a professionally strong public dental offer, i.e. children and young people, the elderly, people under public care and the most vulnerable patients. The Labour Party is engaged in a good division of tasks between the state, the regionally elected level and the municipalities that ensure the best possible services for the population. The current successful dental model was introduced in 1984. The aim of the conversion was better management and coordination of resources. Prior to 1984, dental services had been characterised by small, vulnerable, single-person clinics with little or no professional environment, major municipal differences, as well as a demanding recruitment situation. The conversion was evaluated by the Bondevik government in 2005 and described as very successful. The evaluation showed that the public dental service had become more accessible, several received a good offer, it became easier to recruit, the professionalism was strengthened, and the service became more cost-effective. The Labour Party believes that the government's proposal will take us back to what was the starting point in 1984, i.e. a cost-effective recommunisation without professional arguments to improve public dental provision for the population. These are proposals that undermine the confidence of politicians. The process becomes even less understandable when the Christian People's Party and the Left in the last hour chose to support the government parties in proposing a trial scheme for up to 15 voluntary municipalities and postponing the transfer to 2023. In addition, they split the dental environment by legislating that dental nurses — one part of the important health prostheses — should belong to the municipalities and that the specialist dental provision should also be transferred to the municipalities. Dental health is an important part of health. The Labour Party considers it irresponsible to carry out such a major health reform on such a weak professional basis and such a random and little systematic process. This does not give the predictability that will further develop the dental offer. It does not ensure equivalent dental provision for the whole population, but will fragment both the services and the professional environment and weaken the dental provision. The working party sees no need to change a scheme that has proven that it is working. There are no professional or organisational arguments to improve dental health. Therefore, we do not support the proposed changes that result in the transfer of responsibility for dental care to the municipalities. The Labour Party believes that a regionally elected level is far better equipped to take responsibility for dental care in the future than municipalities with wide variations in population and resource base. The Workers' Party will further develop the public dental service under county ownership.

The most important thing is that it is where good health is created. Health services repair unhealth, while schools, kindergartens, family protection and safe local environments create health. That's why local politicians are doctors, not medical doctors, but social doctors. Local policies have a greater impact on the health of people than much of what is happening in healthcare. The second important reason for doing such extensive and complex tasks for municipalities is that local politicians and municipalities can think entirely from a local perspective. That's wise. It was going to prevent silos, and it was going to help create a more coherent healthcare service where different professionals worked as a team around the patient. The municipalities have to a lesser extent succeeded in doing so. Nursing, home care, physiotherapist, health centre, school health service, psychologist, PPT, health centres, everyday rehabilitation team – I could still list – but you understand the point. Local authorities have been given more and more demanding health tasks, but they have organized them in silos. It cannot continue. Therefore, the government presented the primary health message. Later today, the Stortinge will join in extensive legislative proposals such as a follow-up to this Communication, to make demands for a wide range of competences and to facilitate the future take-over of dental services by municipalities. The major municipal reform in the 1960s was the result of municipal responsibility for the nine-year primary school. In my opinion, this municipal reform is a result of the extensive tasks that the municipalities have already received within the primary health service area. This is well demonstrated in the position of the Health and Care Committee on the primary health notification. There, the majority of the committee – and I stress – from the Labour Party, the Right, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left write the following: ‘...shows that a new municipal structure with a larger trade union environment can improve the ability of municipalities to provide high-quality health and care services in line with current and future needs. The notification can therefore be seen in connection with Notifying. St. 14 Municipal Reform – new tasks for larger municipalities.’ [[2014-2015]] The reality is that, as a consequence of the interaction reform, primary health service tasks have already grown out of many of today’s municipalities. A very clear example of this is the requirement for municipal emergency 24 hours. These are emergency beds that replace unnecessary hospitalisations and require multidisciplinary competence. They are now being expanded to include mental health and dependence. Many of these beds are now established in intercommunal solutions. The requirement to have psychological competence and midwife is also solutions that we see are established intermunicipally. Responsibility for the main welfare tasks is shifted from the local government hall to intercommunal structures. That is what is at issue today. Are we still going to have welfare municipalities in Norway, which are responsible for the largest part of healthcare, and which in the future will require more complex and higher competences, where professionals do not want to be left alone in their areas, but work in teams with others? If the answer to that is yes, today is the first step in a comprehensive community reform. That's my answer, and that's the government's answer. If the answer is no, those who vote no today must also answer who will be responsible for the primary health service in the future. For the task has already grown out of many of today's municipalities. Adult pain increases every year. Takes over, for example: the state or regions of these tasks, then we lose something valuable in the Norwegian model. We lose the total responsibility for contributing to good health in society and the greatest responsibility for repairing un-health. Societal doctors will no longer be responsible for the medical doctor. I think that will lead to poorer health, poorer health care and less democracy. That is why this reform, adopted today, is the first step and an incredibly important reform to ensure that we continue to have a good and strong primary health service in Norway, which is, in fact, the largest part of healthcare, and which solves the greatest health challenges in our society.

I live in a municipality that has chosen to join two neighbouring municipalities. A year ago, Asker, Hurum and Røken agreed that they would become a new large municipality. It was a comprehensive and good process before the decision was taken by the three local authorities. Now it is working well across the municipal borders so that the new Asker municipality can be operational from 1 January 2020. I would like to pay great tribute to the three rapporteurs and the political leadership of the three municipalities, while also giving tribute to the rapporteurs and political leadership of all other municipalities around the country who have decided to join together, for there have been extensive processes. It is demanding to stand in front and plan for a development that is believed to be best for the municipality and its inhabitants in a long-term perspective. There have been many considerations to take and many opinions that have gone in several directions. There's also been a lot of emotion, because most of us have strong feelings for our municipality, and there's nothing new that there's resistance to change. There has also been a lot of fear propaganda, but when people realize that the municipality and the community don't disappear at a municipal merger, they get calmer. The centre party has almost made people believe it with their centralization rhetoric. It's like we're still living where we live, in the same neighborhood and in the same local environment. We're going to have the same proximity center, the school will still be called the same and standing there. It will also include the health home, the football team and other important neighbourhood sites and institutions. The change is that municipalities are growing, gaining wider environments of competence and will be better able to provide good, equivalent services to the inhabitants. Asker is a large municipality with 60,000 inhabitants, but although my municipality is what is called robust, a technical report shows that the new large municipality Asker, with the smoke and Hurum, will be better equipped to meet the challenges of the future than if Asker would continue as before alone – especially in land and housing policy. The expected population growth in our area will pose environmental and transport challenges. A common land policy for the three municipalities where housing, industry and recreation are seen in the context will strengthen the potential for a more sensible and better use of land and for more environmentally friendly transport solutions. The winners of the municipal reform are the inhabitants. New, strong municipalities can ensure better services. Fortunately, the local reform is not over today. It's in process. Someone needs more time. Right will continue to encourage and contribute with good economic incentives. Today’s government is the guarantor that the process continues and that reform work is not reversed. That is why the elections in September are important.

The municipal reform is the result of a substantial change in Norway over the 50 years since the last major change in the municipal structure. Norway has changed for communication purposes. We have taken the step into digital reality – changes few, if any, in 1964 could imagine. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e4780	I would first like to thank the proposers for raising an important debate. The southern Sami language is important in the Sami culture, and we are today in a situation where the southern Sami language is in a critical situation. The work on strengthening the Sami languages has been a priority task over a longer period of time. This applies to Sami languages in general, but it is particularly important for the small language groups, such as Southern Sami and Lulesamian. The Sami languages are cultural and important in the continuation of Sami values and knowledge. The right to use Sami in meeting with the public and the right to training in and in Sami is one of the most important rights for people together. This is therefore a theme that both the Right and the Government have been concerned about, and on this basis, in consultation with the Sameting, a private public Sámi language selection was launched in 2014. The task of the selection was to review current schemes, measures and legislation relating to the Sami languages, and to assess how these can be adapted to the current public sector organisation and ensure functional and equivalent public services in Sami. The selection should also provide suggestions on how the public sector can secure staff with skills in Sami languages and look at schemes and measures to encourage more people to learn and use the Sami languages. The report for the current selection, NOU 2016:18, The Heart Language, was recently out of hearing, and the government is now working on how to follow up the recommendations contained in the report. In this context, I refer to the Council of State's letter to the Committee, where he writes that it is too early to decide how the NOU on Sami languages should be followed up and how the matter should possibly be referred to the Stortinget. Right is the sum of this. The proposers point to an important point, namely that the sectoral principle leads to a weakening of the country’s governance of the common policy. Therefore, the Government Party and the Cooperation Parties have become aware that the Government is considering a possible new budget model for the Sameting. We look forward to the further discussions between the Department and the Sameting, and hope that they will agree on a model that will ensure coordination and sound political priorities. I am pleased that the Committee in this matter has a unified position asking the government in the follow-up of NOU 2016:18, the heart language, to have a special note in Southern Sami language and the links between language, culture and nutrition.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e4805	I am co-rapporteur on this proposal together with representatives from the Workers' Party and the SV, where we are asking to present a separate report on Southern Sami language, culture and industry. The Sami population has several languages and is scattered. Today, only a few hundred people speak Southern Sami. In other words, the language is under strong pressure, and that is why we are promoting this proposal for a representative. Language is characterised as a strongly threatened language. In the last decade there has been a revitalization of Sami language. This has also helped to make Southern Sami culture visible. Several municipalities, such as Snåsa, Røyrvik and Hattfjelldal, are or have applied for inclusion in the Sami language area. In the autumn of 2016, Røros opened its own Southern Saami kindergarten. Uncertainty has also been created, including: through cuts in the allocations to the Southern Sam Sam School in Hattfjelldal and inertia in the continuation of the plans of the museum and the cultural centre Saemien Sijte in Snåsa. In autumn 2016, the Sami language selection delivered its report, the heart language, to the government. The selection has gone through legislation, measures and schemes in southern Sami, lulesamic and northern Sami languages in Norway. The report stresses that Sami languages are endangered languages and that more users are needed to keep the languages alive. The measures proposed include the strengthening of Sami languages in kindergartens and schools and more people with a common language competence in public services. It is also pointed out that distance learning in schools and distance consultations in the health and care sector need to be organised more closely. We stress, as proposers, that it is important to follow up the thorough work done in the Sami Language Committee. We are clear that there is no contradiction between the implementation of measures mentioned in the Heart Language Report and the provision of a key message for a comprehensive review of measures for Southern Sami languages, culture and industry. I would like to point out that the Committee had meetings with representatives of the Sameting in the context of this matter. The representatives of Sametinget stressed that the situation in the Southern Sami language area had to be seen in the context of and addressed together with measures in the Sami language in general through the follow-up to the Heart Language Report. I agree with that, and I believe that the proposals we have made support this. I also refer to our remarks. I have to say I was a little surprised when there was a great lack of enthusiasm from the representatives of Sameting. I got the understanding that they didn't think there was a need for extra attention just about the southern Sami. The southern Sami is a minority in the minority and therefore has particularly major challenges. Today we have received a letter from Sametinget where they have a completely different view of this, and where they see the need for extra attention around the Southern Sami. We now have the opportunity to change and vote in favour of that proposal if we want to follow the whole thing's view as presented today. This is about more than language. It's about cultural development, it's about cultural conservation, the museum in Snåsa, for example. the storage capacity of Southern Sami articles. It is also about the development of industry, for example: the unsustainable predatory situation in these areas. The southern Sami reindeer is the support of the southern Sami culture. What distinguishes these two proposals is essentially whether we want this to be a big message of our own, or whether it should be a follow-up in the follow-up to the heart language. The minority in this setting therefore believe that a single big message is the best, but I am at least glad that we have come to the conclusion that the committee agrees that this should have its own attention in the follow-up to the heart language.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e4965	The Committee has examined the proposals made by the government to legislate on the requirements of the Health and Welfare Services Act, to legislate on the local health service’s obligation to provide guidance to specialist health services, to clarify the responsibilities for patients, to use and related training in the municipal health service, to clarify the responsibilities of the municipality for preventing, detecting and averting violence and sexual abuse, to clarify the municipality’s obligation to provide psychosocial protection and follow-up, to remove the requirement for referral to treatment by physiotherapist — and to transfer the responsibility for dental services from the county to the municipality. This is a bill that contains a number of changes, and the committee has worked well on this. Of course, it is the proposal to transfer dental services to municipalities that have received the most attention, both in consultation opinions to the Committee, in the media debate and in our work. The background to the proposal is the community reform and the need to look critically at the current division of tasks between our elected levels. The dental service has border areas against municipal services, and the government believes that organising and integrating public dentistry services with municipal health promotion and prevention in the health and care services in the municipalities would be a good effect. To put it simply, the municipality is responsible for the entire body, while the teeth are the county municipalities that are over. However, it is a fact that this change has gained a lot of criticism in the hearing opinions as well. In the Committee's work, this has had an impact. A majority today, consisting of the government parties Right and Advance Party and the cooperation parties Christian People's Party and Left, therefore adopt a probationary system in which about 15 municipalities from 2020 can take over dental care. This will be a voluntary scheme, but also a complete task transfer. The testing scheme shall be followed up to 2023. Based on the experience gained from the testing scheme and changes in the municipal structure in Norway after 2020, the majority agree that further expansion to several municipalities can only take place after 2023. Until a new decision is taken, regional responsibility for dental care is continued, except for municipalities in the testing scheme. The majority have assessed that there may be several arguments to put in place a voluntary testing scheme for those municipalities that wish to take over the responsibility for dental care from 2020. The municipal reform has resulted in mutual decisions on a wide range of municipal mergers, and this process will continue over time. In 2023, there will probably have been even more mergers than would have been the case in 2020. Dental responsibility is one of the areas that can be very natural for large municipalities to take over in the long term, and also for others. The four cooperation parties are also concerned that dental competence needs to be built up in the municipalities. Tooth care professionals have important competences in the preventive dental field, and with an increasing ageing population good oral and dental hygiene will be important for a good old age. The law of the Stortinget today also contains positive simplifications such as the fact that patients do not have to walk the road to regularity in order to receive benefit for treatment by a physiotherapist. It has been important for governments and cooperation parties during this period to eliminate unnecessary diversions in order to obtain rapid health care. We also agree that municipalities must have the core competence to ensure high-quality health care. When the previous government introduced the interaction reform, it meant that municipalities would take considerably more responsibility for health and care services for several patient groups. It requires increased competence, and we have always felt it was very unfortunate that the requirements of competence were weakened in the bill, compared to day-to-day legislation. Competence and management are essential for good services, and lack of this represents a challenge in the provision of municipal services. Today's decision to introduce skills requirements could also prevent them, on the one hand, from the wide variation in skills and quality that we see in municipalities today, and, on the other hand, from many warnings about the introduction of professional neutrality. Competence requirements are supported by the large organisations, but it is not supported by the red-green parties, and I expect these parties to elaborate on why later in the debate.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e4980	Today’s dental model has produced good results in terms of better dental health in the population, increased focus on prevention, well decentralised provision and strengthening research and competence. The dental service is actually one of the services the population is most satisfied with, and, as the rapporteur said, there was an unusually high interest in the particular dental part of what we are dealing with in this matter. It turns out that all the heavy trade unions, all the large organizations, strongly advise against a transfer of dental services to the municipalities. The Labour Party therefore considers that it is alarming that the government is still choosing to propose such a transfer, which is based neither on heavy professional or organisational arguments, nor will it offer a better offer to those who need a strong public dental offer, that is to say, all children and young people, the elderly, those who are under public care and the most vulnerable patients. The good dental health of children and young people and different priority groups is a result of systematic effort over many years. Regional centres of excellence have been established to strengthen the practical competence and the provision of specialist health services. One of the results of this competence environment is that the dental environment is actually those in health that are best able to detect and send care failure messages. It is still unclear to the Workers' Party how this will be organised in the future when it is to be transferred to 350 municipalities – whether it is still to be intermunicipal, something that is really being argued against when it comes to building strong municipalities. The Christian People's Party and the Left had the opportunity to provide a majority in the Grand Duchy who quite clearly said no to the transfer of the public dental offer from the regional elected level to about 350 municipalities. Instead, they create uncertainty, confusion and unforeseeability, according to what the Workers' Party believes, thus weakening the public dental offer. Christian People's Party and Left support government parties in transferring responsibility but postponing implementation to 2023 and will have a probationary system for up to 15 voluntary municipalities. The dental specialist field must also be transferred to 350 municipalities. It is argued that this would be beneficial for people with extensive and complex needs. For the Workers' Party, it is important that these patients receive the highest and best professional offer, as has been the organisation of the specialist health service in all other health fields. It is also unclear what will happen to the six regional centres of excellence – how they will coordinate themselves with the odontological environments at university level in the future. The majority also split the public dental provision by legislating that municipalities should have dental nurses. Today, dental care providers are responsible for an important part of public dental care, not least in prevention, a field that should be given increased attention. Instead, the entire public dentistry field is being put on hold and must use resources to re-communalise without seeing any evidence of professional or organisational impact. This does not provide predictability to further develop the dental offer. It does not guarantee an equivalent dental provision for the whole population, but it fragmented both the services and the professional environment and weakens the dental provision. Therefore, the Workers' Party does not support the proposed changes resulting in the transfer of responsibility for the public dental services to 350 municipalities. As regards the rest of the subject area that is discussed and discussed in this case, Tove Karoline Knutsen will explain this in a later article.

Access to the right competences is a prerequisite for municipalities to provide necessary and sound health and care services. Prosperity neutrality, which was the mantra of the previous government, I believe is wrong. We need clear, proud professionals in the primary health service. Among other things, we see: the quality of the services of older people varies too much, both between municipalities and within municipalities. We have therefore started the Life Reform throughout our lives, the aim of which is for all older people to receive good services wherever they live. Legislation on the competence of municipal health and care services will be an important contribution, among other things. in this work. Increased competence and multi-disciplinaryism are also a prerequisite for developing good solutions, multidisciplinary cooperation and team organisation. I am therefore pleased that the majority supports the proposal to legislate on competence requirements. Municipal responsibility for health and care services is extensive and complex. Municipalities must have access to a wide range of staff, with different competences, in order to provide necessary and sound services. The legalisation of competence requirements will not be exhaustive as to the type of competence or professional qualifications to be provided by each municipality. In addition to the core competence that legalisation will entail, the municipality itself has to assess the type of personnel it needs to attach to fulfil its responsibilities for a sound and necessary health service. In the proposal, I propose to legislate a guidance obligation for the municipal health and care services and the specialist health service and to clarify the municipality's responsibility for patient, user and family training. It is proposed to clarify the responsibilities of the service for preventing, detecting and averting violence and sexual abuse. The law also clarifies the municipal responsibility for psychosocial preparedness and follow-up. These are also proposals to which the majority subscribe. In line with the wishes of the majority of the population for the treatment of the task message and the primary health service notification, the government proposed changes to the laws transferring public dental care from the county municipalities to the municipalities. The proposition stated that such a task transfer would take place from 2020 onwards. However, the majority of the Committee has asked for a voluntary testing scheme for municipalities wishing to transfer responsibility for the public dentistry at the earliest from 1 January 2020 onwards. The testing scheme shall allow about 15 municipalities to take over the responsibility for the public dental service from that point on. Participation shall be based on voluntary participation by the municipalities and a complete transfer of care-care responsibilities for both the specialist dental and the general dentistry services shall take place. The government also asks to assess the need for financial incentives, so that several municipalities will apply to take over dental services in the testing scheme. The majority have also requested that the scheme be monitored until 2023, and that the government start work on building up dental skills in the municipalities. On this basis, the majority have requested that the government return with its own case assessing the transfer of the overall dental care service based on the experience gained from the trial scheme and which may provide the basis for any decision to transfer public dental care from 2023 at the earliest. This is an important matter. The legislative proposal adopted today provides an important legal basis for this. I am convinced that the experience of the trial scheme will show that it is absolutely correct that the primary health service and the municipality also have a responsibility for dental care. Not least, it is important to create a closeness to the rest of the primary health services in municipalities and a closeness to those users that we see today are not reached by the public dentistry but who have rights under the law. Thus, the pilot scheme will make a good contribution to understanding why such reform will be necessary in the future.

A requirement is introduced that when providing such information, the child be briefed. Children who are related to siblings who are patients with mental illness, drug dependence or serious somatic disease or injury, or who are left behind after parents or siblings who die may have special needs for information and follow-up as children who are related to parents. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e5375	Several of the changes proposed and adopted today will lead to a strengthening and clarification of children’s and young people’s rights as patients and users. Two of the most important proposals are a direct result of the commitment to BarnsBest and the Change Factory, which early took up with me the challenges of today's law, and I'm glad they've been heard in this context. The balance for different reasons lies behind several of the changes in the patient and user rights law as regards children and the participation and participation of young people. I believe that there are important reasons why the general rule should still be that both children and young people and parents should be informed when health and care services are provided to children and young people under 16. Parental responsibility includes providing care and care and provides both the right and the obligation to make decisions on behalf of and in consultation with children and young people. In most cases, parents have the best assumptions for this. For children and young people, it is therefore usually quite natural and desirable to inform their parents. Its younger children are, the greater is the need for information and proper care and follow-up. At the same time, a derogation should be introduced from this obligation to inform parents even when children are under 12 years of age, in addition to the current exemption from the obligation to inform their parents when they are between 12 and 16 years of age, and have reasons for this which should be respected. It is important to provide for such derogations when health professionals are informed that children may be in a serious situation, where the best consideration for the child is that the information is not passed on to their parents. The exemption is limited to cases where serious consideration of the child is opposed to informing the parents. The Health Directorate will provide information and guidance on the exemption by updating the circular and posting information on its websites. Nevertheless, it must be emphasised that it is incredibly important to facilitate wise and good practice, so that this derogation helps vulnerable children to dry up and provide information on the situation, so that they can get help to get out of it. The change of laws is important to create a foundation for this, but there is a change in service in practice in dealing with children, creating trust with the child and ensuring that the child can speak openly in difficult situations, which are crucial to our success. The Committee also proposes that vaccination in line with the pediatric vaccination programme should be provided directly by the Patient and User Rights Act as a part of the day-to-day regular care of the child. It is sufficient that one of the parents, or others with parental responsibility, consent to vaccination. Child vaccination is carefully tested, safe and recommended and allows us in Norway to control many infectious diseases that were prevalent in the past. This is a good clarification of the law, adopted today. In 2009, provisions of the Health and Special Health Services Act were introduced to ensure that health professionals identify and meet the information and follow-up needs of minor children as a result of being related to parents who are patients with mental illness, drug dependence or serious somatic illness or injury. Children'sBest early brought up with me that minor children who are related to the sibling with such serious health problems, or left behind after parents and siblings who die, have the same needs. The proposals shall contribute to ensuring that health professionals provide the necessary assistance and support in a systematic and sound manner to this vulnerable and vulnerable group of children. The proposals I have now mentioned, as well as several of the other proposals, will help to strengthen the legal position of children and young people. The perspective of children and young people will be even better taken care of when they receive health and care services. I believe this is a quite natural development of our legislation, where we now make it clear in several areas the child's independent role in dealing with health care. These are important legislative changes, which are adopted, hopefully, later today.

We consider to look at the proposal of the Green Party on this issue, but we do not regard it as part of this tax debate, but as part of the greater regulation of the aquaculture industry in general.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e5659	The main challenge facing the Norwegian farming industry is the environmental impact of the industry, both the environmental impact on the industry and the impact of the industry itself. This is a problem that has been promised to be solved for very many years, and the solution is still not coming. Of course, the Green Party sees, as far as I see it, that it is a reasonable principle that a fair share of the value-added in the farming industry, a breeding industry that produces huge profits, must fall to the municipalities and local communities that provide land and resources and that must take on the burdens of the industry. The fee shall provide clear incentives for conversion to safe closed installations without the release of lice, waste and chemicals.’;» A major feature of such a charge becomes natural enough that, at least during a transitional period, the charge has as its primary objective the removal of environmental problems. As a result, when the installation has solved the problems, closed operation or a similar good technology will be reduced and eliminated. An investigation of such an environmental tax should also include a plan for ensuring long-term predictability and reasonable income for municipalities, for example. through a State compensation or another form of expenditure that can be determined. As far as the proposal promoted by the Right and the Advance Party is concerned, it is not so far from what we have proposed, but it is so little ambitious on the environmental side and so traditional in its priority of growth as the main objective that we are leaving to vote in favour of it. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e6234	In 2015, Europe, not just Norway, experienced an exceptional situation with a high number of asylum seekers that neither we nor other countries were prepared to deal with. To get the situation under control, we made a refugee settlement with the Right, the Advance Party, the Left, the Christian People's Party and the Center Party on 19 November of the same year. We in the Workers' Party stretched far to find a common solution, over old disagreements. The same was true of most other conciliators. The agreement meant that the government would conduct different types of investigations and return to Stortinget with matters in defined areas. One of the assignments we're dealing with today. One main question was whether the level of social benefits in Norway was such that a disproportionate proportion of the refugees sought residence here in the country. In addition, there was a need to look at living requirements for non-supporting benefits for work and activity, and there was a need to look at activity and generation requirements where it was natural. A review of the social security rights of asylum seekers and refugees was notified in Prop. 1 S Appendix 1 for 2015-2016. The working party took note of this. When the matter is now on the table, it turns out that there is a matter of general cuts in the social benefits for refugees, but the government has not responded to the task that Stortinget gave. The main question was whether it was possible to demonstrate a link between the level of social benefits in Norway and a disproportionate proportion of asylum seekers in Norway, compared with other countries. The Ministry emphasises that Norwegian welfare schemes should not be designed to lead to ‘distortions in migration flows, by providing asylum seekers with incentives to choose Norway as a destination country’. The schemes must further ‘stimulate those who are allowed to stay in Norway to be economically active’. But the government has either jumped over this premise, or tried and failed to demonstrate such a connection. Nevertheless, there is a case of cutting across the whole line, mainly as a mixture of increased living requirements, combined with the lifting of refugees’ exemptions from the requirement to live. As the rapporteur has explained, the proposal also contains some other proposals. In the case of auxiliaries, it is proposed that current living requirements be deleted. The Labour Party supports it. It is important that refugees — and others involved — are not prevented from participating in work, education and activities. There are also some benefits where there is no requirement for prior living, and where it is not proposed to introduce it, so I will not spend time commenting on them. But then we have the cash support. It is proposed to introduce a five-year residence requirement. We support that, since, in our opinion, the cash support is an obstacle, both in terms of women's professional activity and in terms of sending children to kindergarten, and in that way it seems negative in terms of integration. However, we do not support increasing the requirement to live from three to five years for social benefits to single dependants, nor does it support an increase to five years for old age pensions or disability benefits. The old age pension applies to those who are not working age. Disadvantages apply to those who are unable to work due to illness, injury or negligence. Increased living requirements are also proposed for redundancy benefits. We do not support that. Work clearance is just about activity, hopefully with work as a goal. Having to wait several years for this will only inhibit, not promote, integration. In addition, the government proposes to refer elderly and disabled refugees to supplementary benefits for short-stay residents. We do not support that. The majority of adults have already cut twice in this amount of benefit during this period. This proposal will only help to produce poverty, and we do not have to. Nor do we support the proposal to repeal Section 3 of the Asylum Seeker’s Code on Earnings in the Asylum Seeker’s Period if permanent residence is granted. This applies to transitional benefits, disability benefits and pensions and to the calculation of disability benefits and pensions. It is not fair that one group of the population should not be left for what they have earned. Finally, almost none of the 44 hearing bodies support the government's proposal to cut the social benefits of refugees. On the contrary, several heavy authorities believe that the proposals will only contribute to increasing differences between people. Instead, measures on language, increased labour skills and anti-discrimination are requested. It is a more dangerous road, even in the opinion of the Workers' Party.
