ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e6299	In this case, the government follows up the refugee agreement at Stortinget from November 2015. We propose to abolish the specific social security rules for refugees in the area of popular security. At the same time, it is proposed to increase the living requirement to five years for a number of social security benefits. The rights of asylum seekers and refugees in the Norwegian social security system have remained largely unchanged since 1967, although both working life, benefits, social security rights and, in particular, asylum and immigration policies and quantities have changed considerably. The UN Convention on Refugees implies that Norway has an obligation to allow refugees to benefit from popular protection so that they can gain rights. It's not about this discussion. However, the benefits of the refugees in the Norwegian population are significantly beyond our obligations. This means that refugees are entitled to pensions, disability benefits, redundancy benefits, transitional benefits and other benefits even if the normal conditions of the Act on Membership and Social Security are not met. In other words, the requirements for obtaining the same benefits are more stringent for the Nordics than for other types of new nationals. I think the reasons are thin for having these arrangements when we have some hundred or a few thousand a year, but as it comes in tens of thousands of times every year, it's actually urgent to change these rules. That was why the Storting asked the Government in the refugee settlement to start work with a view to introducing housing requirements for the receipt of cash support and benefits that do not support work and activity, and asked the Government to: ‘to review specific arrangements for refugees and asylum-seekers in the public interest and to ensure that activity and earnings requirements are imposed on them where it is natural to do so, with a view to promoting them for the purposes of the Storting as quickly as possible.’;» So what we're doing now. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e6493	As many refugees arrived in Norway and Europe in the autumn of 2015, our systems were tested. This was true both of our immediate reception systems and of our long-term immigration and integration policies. If we're going to be able to do our part in the international effort to receive people on the run, we need robust systems that get people working, and that help to achieve good integration. In connection with the refugee agreement here at Stortinget, the government was given the task of reviewing the various social security schemes that we have, to see if there is a need to make changes that ensure efficient and correct incentives for work. I want to thank the government for doing this job, which is the background to the proposition we have for treatment today. And so it is that the Left cannot support the multiple of the proposals that have been made. The main reason is that we fear that the sum of these proposals will lead to a group that is already poorer than the poorer, and because we have not seen evidence that the various proposals will particularly contribute to increased activity. Some of the proposals, however, are good and constructive and will be supported by the Left. It applies to the proposal to remove outage requirements for access to facilities, and it applies to the proposal to impose outage requirements in order to increase cash support. The left is in principle opposed to the entire cash support scheme, including: because it prevents integration, participation and equality. This also implies that there should be at least a front-end requirement before one can receive it. This was also one of the few concrete points of agreement in the refugee agreement. On the other hand, the proposal to transfer elderly and disabled refugees from old age and disability pensions to supplementary benefits does not receive our support. We can hardly see what the purpose of this is, except to create more bureaucracy and a weak social security network. Nor does the proposal to impose out-of-court obligations to allow refugees to benefit from important schemes such as: support to a single mother or father, the Left sees as peculiarly reasonable. We have an open mind to discuss the various suggestions in the proposition, but we have chosen to reject most of them in line with the option. This is based on the actual realities of the various proposals, but also on the fact that much of the underlying argument is not particularly good. Firstly, the principle of non-discrimination between refugees and other groups is not particularly effective. Comparing someone who has a deliberate choice of moving to Norway to work or bow with someone who comes here because they're hunting for escape is not a good comparison. People who voluntarily move between countries must be able to expect to be able to run a certain level of planning in terms of social security networks and income, and they should accept certain requirements for butid etc. before they can benefit from the welfare schemes to which they have not paid. But refugees, who have tended to earn welfare rights and income in their homelands and then lost everything they owned and were forced to flee, are simply not reasonable to have the same expectations. Equal treatment is to treat the same case equally, but a refugee and a work immigrant is simply not the same case. Secondly, it is difficult to see that the government in this proposition has demonstrated some particular degree of so-called ‘pull’ effect with Norwegian welfare arrangements. In theory, of course, it can vote, but in reality, it is hardly possible that there is transitional aid that determines whether in a desperate situation, people should choose to sit in a step-by-step rubber boat and make a dangerous journey across the Mediterranean. The left is concerned that we should pursue a good integration policy in Norway. The most important thing is that we have an introduction programme that works, that language training is good, that we contribute to sustainable urban development, and not least that refugees are able to get to work as quickly as possible. I would like the government to concentrate on strengthening this work in the future, so that we can continue to welcome people to our country who are taking a new chance and who can contribute with their skills and experience only they get the opportunity.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e6521	In 2015, many refugees came to Europe. Only Norway had more than 31,000 refugees. This was also the background for the refugee settlement here at Stortinget on 19 November 2015. Through 18 points, the Labour Party, the Right, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party, the Centre Party and the Left asked the government to take action. In other words, there was a broad majority standing behind. I record that the Workers' Party, the Christian People's Party, the Centre Party and the Left are no longer subject to the premises and the clear intentions of several of the decisions of the refugee settlement. It's e.g. difficult to recognise the refugee settlement in the Workers' Party's description in point 1.2 of the setting. An attempt is being made to create the impression that the agreement only asked for a ‘overpass’, to ‘map’ and ‘assess’, for the sake of the overview. However, the agreement was exemplaryly clear and concrete, with clear requests for action in a particular direction. It also made the follow-up to decisions easier. In paragraph 6 of the agreement, the government included: asked to ‘ensure that the level of benefits for asylum seekers should be such that Norway does not appear economically attractive in relation to comparable European countries’. In paragraph 16, the government was asked to ‘start work for the introduction of living requirements for the receipt of cash support and benefits that do not support work and activity’. In paragraph 17, the government was asked to ensure that activity and earnings requirements are imposed on specific schemes for refugees and asylum seekers where this is natural, with a view to promoting this for the Storting as soon as possible. That's exactly what a bill has now been put forward. It is proposed to wind up most of the specific provisions of popular fear for refugees, which today provide refugees with full minimum benefits from social security, regardless of their length of residence in the country. It is proposed that the most vulnerable groups, namely disabled persons and persons over 67 years of age, be guaranteed an equivalent benefit through the State scheme of supplementary social benefits. In other words, the proposals that have been put forward balance the different considerations I perceive that the Storting is concerned about. Furthermore, it is proposed to raise the living requirements from three to five years for pensions, disability benefits, redundancy benefits and social benefits for a single mother or father. Furthermore, it is proposed to introduce a five-year housing requirement for the right to cash support. On the other hand, it is proposed to abolish the three-year residence requirement for rights to facilities, including: to strengthen the possibility of work. In the light of the hearing opinions, the proposal has been adjusted on some points. The government is committed to tightening up the benefits in order to bring them closer to the schemes in other European countries, in order to ensure that the beneficiaries have an adequate connection with the Reich. At the same time, the government has been concerned that proposals should not be made at the expense of people's ability to live good and safe lives, nor should large costs be passed on to municipalities. The proposed mitigations would help to facilitate a humane, sober refugee policy and ensure the long-term sustainability of the schemes. There will now be significantly fewer refugees. This does not mean that the basis for making adjustments has lapsed. The review has shown that the schemes are not appropriate and that they are adapted at a different time. Regardless of the number that comes, it is necessary to have social security legislation that is equipped for proper management, not only of those who have already arrived, but also of any future situations of exceptional inflow. I am pleased that a Joint Committee endorses the proposal to abolish the requirement to have a residence permit for auxiliary rights and that there is a majority in the Committee on the proposal to introduce a residence requirement for the right to cash support. I can only take note that the Workers' Party, the Christian People's Party, the Centre Party and the Left have now left important parts of the refugee settlement. Finally, I note that this means that Norway will continue to be at the top of the world with regard to the economic conditions we offer asylum seekers and refugees, with schemes far better than those offered to other groups in Norway, including those who have come on a humanitarian basis, Norwegian citizens with long stays abroad.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e6831	Work clearance allowance, AAP, is a temporary benefit which shall not exceed what is necessary to bring the beneficiary into or back to work or to clarify whether the conditions for receiving disability benefit are met. The main picture is that the AAP scheme did not lead to a shorter duration of social security than was the case in the past when the AAP was three benefits: rehabilitation, resettlement and temporary unpaid pay. One main problem seems to be that the follow-up of the individual is not good enough. The Christian People's Party supports the intention to clarify in its intended purpose that the scheme is a health-related performance, but believes that this clarification requires an offer to those who are not defined as having a claim to the performance. The Christian People's Party also believes that removing the opportunities for those who do not have a long residence in Norway to receive redundancy benefits will hardly help more people to get into work, but may help to keep more people out of work. The Government proposes to legislate the practice of the State of employment and welfare on the delineation of foreign residence for the recipients of redundancy benefits. This is about legislating current practices. However, legislation could make it more difficult for the employment and welfare movement to exercise discretion in the payment of redundancy payments. That is why the Christian People's Party is critical of that proposal. A number of objections were raised to the mobility requirement proposed by the government. The Christian People's Party believes that it should be the individual's situation and state of health that is essential for the appropriateness of requiring the removal of workers' redundancy payments. If not, the possibility of work can be reduced rather than strengthened. The government proposes to change the calculation basis for redundancy payments in a way that would reduce the number of people who have tried to get a job in advance of a period of illness by reducing the amount of work. The Christian People's Party is critical of this. We also believe that a standstill period of 52 weeks will be able to cut off people who have a real need for redundancy money, because they are out of work with a background to a significant health problem. The duration of the performance and the extent of exemptions is a particularly important point in this case. On the one hand, it is important that the AAP period is not so short that this leads to increased access to and pressure on disability. If the maximum duration has been reached and the disease that results in reduced working capacity persists, most of them are likely to be granted disability benefit. Having such strict limitations of duration that there are several dissatisfactions will not be worthwhile either for the individual or for society. On the other hand, the scheme should be temporarily more stringent than that of the current rules, with four years as a limitation and liberal exemption rules which provide for additional duration. The likelihood of transition to employment increases significantly as the maximum duration approaches. A tighter amount of social benefits can help to speed up the follow-up of the individual and start-up of the activity, which in turn can lead to faster work. The solution that the Christian People's Party believes best suits both needs is the option that also received the greatest support during the hearing – from 19 hearing bodies, including most municipalities, Nav offices, Specter, Works, YS and subsidized by the Joint Organisation of Disabilities and the Association of Norway's Disabled People's Association. This solution shall have a maximum duration of three years. Exemptions are not limited in time, but are subject to the conditions for exemptions. The central party is proposing this option, and the Christian People's Party will therefore vote in favour of the proposal of the Central Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e6911	Today, proposals for changes to the rules on redundancy pay are being discussed. The proposal will make the system of redundancy payments more labour-oriented. The proposed changes to the laws mean a narrower approach to the scheme, a tightening of the social welfare system, and an increase in labour incentives. In other words, we have raised the threshold into the order. We're reducing the period for how long you can go into the scheme, and we're making sure that the follow-up of those in the scheme is closer. The overall proposal provides a good balance for those who receive redundancy benefits to have a more activity-oriented and labour-oriented social welfare programme, and which facilitates a more flexible transition from redundancy pay to work. The main task of the government is to get more people to work. It is worrying that so many have long periods outside of work. This increases the likelihood of disability and permanent exclusion from work. We also know that the risk of not getting back to work increases with the duration of absence. It has been seven years since the introduction of redundancy pay and the replacement of rehabilitation money, re-routing money and time-limited unpaid pay. An important purpose of the conversion was to reduce the overall duration and increase the transition to work. None of the parts have happened. This is why the government is now proposing changes to the rules on redundancy payments. Some recipients of redundancy benefits need assistance from the State of Employment and Welfare for a short period of time before it is appropriate to try or return to work. Other recipients will need more extensive and long-term assistance. We have clear knowledge that better and more effective social benefits are achieved by initiating work-oriented activity in the past and by carrying out work-oriented activities and medical treatment at the same time. Nearly seven out of ten new recipients of disability benefit come from redundancy benefits. This shows the importance of obtaining more labour-oriented social benefits for recipients of redundancy benefits. The goal is several social cycles that end up with a job rather than disability. The most important step taken with the changes in the law is to reduce the length of time during which work clearances can be received. This will put greater pressure on both the individual recipient and the State of Employment and Welfare to make good use of the benefit period. There is also good evidence that the transition to work is increasing as the maximum duration approaches. The analysis in the notification part of the legislative proposal makes it apparent that the challenges of the system of redundancy payments are partly linked to the legislation of the scheme, but also to the content and quality of the work-oriented offer provided by the establishment. The legislative changes are the first steps forward and the government is pleased that the majority of the committee supports the government's proposals. In order to increase the transition to work and to support the positive effects of a time limit, more frequent follow-up of the recipients through the social welfare cycle is necessary. Youth is a priority group in labour market policy. Young people entering the labour clearance scheme will be given special priority in the follow-up work as a result of the youth effort we put in place in 2017 and where the government of the revised national budget has proposed to further intensify efforts. In the government budget for 2018, the government will return to the question of follow-up of recipients of redundancy payments more generally.

The current eighth and ninth terms are going to be the seventh and eighth terms. the fourth paragraph of Section 20-10 is replaced by the following: The person who, at the age of 67, received disability benefits due to occupational injury receives a full guarantee pension regardless of the provisions on social security periods laid down in the first and second subparagraphs, in so far as the part of the old-age pension corresponding to the invalidity due to the occupational injury is concerned. The second sentence of the second paragraph of Section 20-10 is deleted. II In Law 29 April 2005 No. 21 on the supplementary benefit to persons with short butide in Norway is amended as follows: Section 1 is replaced by the following: § 1 Purpose of law The purpose of the laws is to guarantee a minimum total income for people who have reached the age of 67 and who have lost low or no returns from the population because of short butide in Norway. Section 2 is replaced by the following: With a refugee, a person who has residence permits under Section 28 of the Foreign Affairs Act is in the law here. With an unaccompanied refugee, one in the law here is a refugee who has not reached the age of 67 and who fulfils the conditions of the people's protection laws §§ 12-4 to 12-7. The current first, second and third terms become the second, third and new fourth terms. the first paragraph of Section 3 is replaced by the following: Supplementary social benefits are provided to people who are resident in Norway and who either have reached the age of 67 or are unemployed. A person is considered to be a resident if he/she is registered in the population register and has Norwegian citizenship, or if he/she has obtained permanent residence permits or temporary residence permits that provide the basis for permanent residence permits. Paragraph 4 first and second paragraphs shall be replaced by the following: The right to supplementary benefits falls when a person stays or intends to stay abroad for more than 45 days in conjunction or for more than 45 days in total for a period of benefit, see Section 11. If the rest of the world is intended to last more than 45 days in conjunction, the benefits will decrease from the month following the month of the rest of the world. In other cases, the benefits fall from the month following the month in which the person concerned had been abroad for 45 days or the sum of the stays abroad for a period of 45 days. Section 5 is replaced by the following: § 5 Full supplementary benefit Full supplementary benefit for persons aged 67 years or over shall correspond to: a) the minimum pension level at a high rate for single social security workers and for social security workers with an ecthema who have not reached the age of 67 and who are not unemployed; (b) the minimum level of pension at the normal rate for social security contributors with eczema who have reached 67 years of age or who are unemployed and for social security workers who share housing with their adult children or other adults, without defining the relationship between them as such; [[couple of buildings]] Full supplementary benefit for displaced persons shall correspond to: (a) the minimum annual performance of high-rate unadulterated social security benefits for single social security workers and for social security workers with an eczema which has not reached the age of 67 and has not reached the age of unaccompanied refugee; (b) minimum annual benefits for disabled persons at the normal rate for social security workers with an eczema of 67 years of age or who are unemployed. The same applies to social workers who share housing with their grown children or other adults, without defining the relationship between them as marriage-like. [[couple of buildings]] Section 6 is replaced by the following: § 6 Income to be deducted from supplementary social benefits Full supplementary social benefits shall be reduced by the income of the social security funder as well as by the spouse. However, if the spouse has reached the age of 67, his/her income shall only be included in the test of the benefits of the social security funder with that part exceeding the full supplementary social security benefit referred to in Section 5, first paragraph, point (b). However, if the spouse is not yet a refugee, his/her income shall only be included in the test of the benefits of the social security funder with that part exceeding the full supplementary social security benefit referred to in Section 5, second paragraph, point (b). As income is considered a) labour income (b) pensions, disability benefits and other benefits from people's rights to life support, including allowances; (c) introduction aid for newly arrived immigrants, qualifying aid, cash support and contributions under the Marriage Act; (d) other Norwegian public or private pensions, disability benefits and other life support benefits, including dependant allowances; e) capital income f) corresponding income or benefits referred to in the letters (a) to (e) from the rest of the world. For displaced persons, labour income shall not be lower than expected income after disability, see the third paragraph of § 12-9. the second paragraph of Section 10 is replaced by the following: In the case of a change in the minimum pension level and the basic amount, the supplementary benefit shall be adjusted in line with these changes. Paragraph 10 of the present second paragraph, second sentence, becomes the new third paragraph and is replaced by the following: Deductions in the benefit under Section 6 due to old-age pension or disability benefit from social security, or other benefits directly linked to the basic amount or becoming regulated under the corresponding rules as for old-age pension from social security shall be regulated in line with the change in these benefits. Paragraph 21 second and new third paragraphs are replaced by the following: The employment and welfare seat may require a social security funder to meet in person at the single local office up to four times a year and display passports or other travel documents for the purpose of verifying the right to benefits. The Ministry may provide for measures to verify that the conditions laid down in Section 4 are fulfilled or that a social security funder has resided abroad in breach of these rules. ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e10134	I just want to explain that the Labour Party believes that we should have a vote on whether it should be dealt with directly in the Chamber. The reason for this is that the content of this matter has been thoroughly discussed in the Committee and through lengthy negotiations between the parties which supported the reform of the Grand Duchy. A broad settlement was drawn up with a background in the negotiations. It is clearly designed, with a clear guarantee in the majority observations in the form of a promise to the inhabitants. That matter is, the government's failure to follow this promise. That is what is the basis for the proposal. This means that the Storting should be given the opportunity to give feedback to the government on what was actually the basis of the agreement. Then the different parties can express their view of it. Based on the answers given by the Minister of Justice and the police director in Stortinget and the media, there may seem to be a different perception of the content of the conciliation between the parties and the government. A committee procedure will not clarify such a different opinion beyond what is known, but it will do a plenary session. That is why, in our view, the matter does not need further clarification in a committee, and we therefore ask that voting on the matter be dealt with directly in the Chamber.
ParlaMint-NO_2017-06-08.ana.ud473e10143	I will give an explanation of vote from the Party of Advancement, the Christian People's Party, the Left and the Right. We support the presidency's position in the matter. We believe that this is the best way to deal with the proposal. It will also be fully in line with previous practice here in the Grand Duchy. Although it is late in the period and at the end of the period: there was a similar vote a year ago over a SV representative proposal on the transfer of fishing quotas. It was promoted in Stortinget on 9 June last year, the position of the Business Committee was four days later, and the debate in Stortinget took place one week after the presentation. We have time for that. We then receive regular and robust treatment in the Committee, and the matter is thus also thoroughly clarified before voting.
